          "doc_id": "Pneumatic Separation and Recycling of Anode and Cathode Materials from Spent Lithium Iron Phosphate Batteries",
          "title": "Pneumatic separation and recycling of anode and cathode materials from spent lithium iron phosphate batteries",
          "year": 2024,

        "text_chunk": {
          "chunk_id": "CHUNK_Pneumatic Separation and Recycling of Anode and Cathode Materials from Spent Lithium Iron Phosphate Batteries_Introduction_c5ba2aa0_sub0",
          "text": "## Introduction Recently, Ni-MH and lithium iron batteries as high-energy vehicle power batteries have been developed rapidly because of their advantages such as high energy density, fast charge and discharge process, long cycle life, and non-pollution attributes. However, the battery capacity decreases after hundreds of charge–discharge cycles, which finally leads to the battery being scrapped. From the viewpoint of environmental protection, conservation of natural resources and lowering the cost, battery recycling is necessary. The authors summarize the methods of Ni-MH battery and lithium iron battery recycling in domestic and foreign research, including the hydrometallurgical processing method, the pyrometallurgical processing method and the combined processing method, and each method is evaluated. An overview is then given about secondary pollution, security problems and solutions in the existing methods, which lays a foundation for future recycling of traction batteries (Yu et al., 2014). A shortage of Li material is expected when electric vehicles become extensively used, and new battery technology is needed (Vikström et al., 2013). Minimum recycling rates of 25% by September 2012 and 45% by 2016 are required according to the EU Directive 2006/66/EC. The recycling rate will reach 50%–75%, depending on various types of the batteries (Miedema and Moll, 2013). The recycling processes of spent lithium iron phosphate batteries include thermal treatment, wet treatment, biological treatment and mechanical treatment (Al-Thyabat et al., 2013). Waste toner is dealt with by heat treatment (400°C, 600°C, and 800°C) to obtain carbon-coated ferric oxides, the structures, morphologies and electrochemical performances of which have been subsequently investigated as active materials for lithium iron batteries (Li et al., 2018). Hanisch et al. (2015) used thermal decomposition of the polyvinylidene fluoride binder to lessen the cohesion of coated active material particles and weaken the adhesion between coating and foil. Via incineration and impact liberation, 97.1% w/w of the electrode coating can be regained with aluminum impurities of only 0.1% w/w, 30 times purer than the comparative process. Fernandes et al. (2013) extracted and removed Zn(II), Fe(III), Co(II) and Ni(II) in the leachate with 2-ethylhexylphosphonic acid-2-ethylhexyl monoester (PC-88A). The lanthanide metal was extracted at pH = 1 and the recycling rate was more than 99.9%. Innocenzi and Vegliò (2012) pulverized the nickelhydrogen battery (<500μm) using 2 mol/L H2SO4 at 80 ^∘C for 3 h and 1 mol/L H2SO4 at 25 ^∘C for 1 h to obtain the leachate. The pH value was adjusted to 1.6 with NaOH and the rare earth metal was precipitated using sulfate precipitation. The resulting recycling rate was 99%, containing 64% lanthanide metal sulfate and 28% cerium sulfate. Nevertheless, the wet process requires a large amount of the strong acid, alkali, oxidant and extractant, and resulted in a large amount of waste liquid. Biological treatment with bio-acid instead of traditionally used sulfuric and nitric acids has less impact on the environment. Mishra et al. (2008) used the acidophilic Thiobacillus ferrooxidans to leach the metal in the cathode material of spent lithium iron batteries.",
          "parent_chunk_id": "CHUNK_Pneumatic Separation and Recycling of Anode and Cathode Materials from Spent Lithium Iron Phosphate Batteries_Introduction_c5ba2aa0",
          "section_name": "Introduction Recently, Ni-MH and lithium iron batteries as high-energy vehicle power batteries have been developed rapidly because of their advantages such as high energy density, fast charge and discharge process, long cycle life, and non-pollution attributes. However, the battery capacity decreases after hundreds of charge–discharge cycles, which finally leads to the battery being scrapped. From the viewpoint of environmental protection, conservation of natural resources and lowering the cost, battery recycling is necessary. The authors summarize the methods of Ni-MH battery and lithium iron battery recycling in domestic and foreign research, including the hydrometallurgical processing method, the pyrometallurgical processing method and the combined processing method, and each method is evaluated. An overview is then given about secondary pollution, security problems and solutions in the existing methods, which lays a foundation for future recycling of traction batteries (Yu et al., 2014). A shortage of Li material is expected when electric vehicles become extensively used, and new battery technology is needed (Vikström et al., 2013). Minimum recycling rates of 25% by September 2012 and 45% by 2016 are required according to the EU Directive 2006/66/EC. The recycling rate will reach 50%–75%, depending on various types of the batteries (Miedema and Moll, 2013). The recycling processes of spent lithium iron phosphate batteries include thermal treatment, wet treatment, biological treatment and mechanical treatment (Al-Thyabat et al., 2013). Waste toner is dealt with by heat treatment (400°C, 600°C, and 800°C) to obtain carbon-coated ferric oxides, the structures, morphologies and electrochemical performances of which have been subsequently investigated as active materials for lithium iron batteries (Li et al., 2018). Hanisch et al. (2015) used thermal decomposition of the polyvinylidene fluoride binder to lessen the cohesion of coated active material particles and weaken the adhesion between coating and foil. Via incineration and impact liberation, 97.1% w/w of the electrode coating can be regained with aluminum impurities of only 0.1% w/w, 30 times purer than the comparative process. Fernandes et al. (2013) extracted and removed Zn(II), Fe(III), Co(II) and Ni(II) in the leachate with 2-ethylhexylphosphonic acid-2-ethylhexyl monoester (PC-88A). The lanthanide metal was extracted at pH = 1 and the recycling rate was more than 99.9%. Innocenzi and Vegliò (2012) pulverized the nickelhydrogen battery (<500μm) using 2 mol/L H2SO4 at 80 ^∘C for 3 h and 1 mol/L H2SO4 at 25 ^∘C for 1 h to obtain the leachate. The pH value was adjusted to 1.6 with NaOH and the rare earth metal was precipitated using sulfate precipitation. The resulting recycling rate was 99%, containing 64% lanthanide metal sulfate and 28% cerium sulfate. Nevertheless, the wet process requires a large amount of the strong acid, alkali, oxidant and extractant, and resulted in a large amount of waste liquid. Biological treatment with bio-acid instead of traditionally used sulfuric and nitric acids has less impact on the environment. Mishra et al. (2008) used the acidophilic Thiobacillus ferrooxidans to leach the metal in the cathode material of spent lithium iron batteries.",

          "text": "## Introduction Recently, Ni-MH and lithium iron batteries as high-energy vehicle power batteries have been developed rapidly because of their advantages such as high energy density, fast charge and discharge process, long cycle life, and non-pollution attributes. However, the battery capacity decreases after hundreds of charge–discharge cycles, which finally leads to the battery being scrapped. From the viewpoint of environmental protection, conservation of natural resources and lowering the cost, battery recycling is necessary. The authors summarize the methods of Ni-MH battery and lithium iron battery recycling in domestic and foreign research, including the hydrometallurgical processing method, the pyrometallurgical processing method and the combined processing method, and each method is evaluated. An overview is then given about secondary pollution, security problems and solutions in the existing methods, which lays a foundation for future recycling of traction batteries (Yu et al., 2014). A shortage of Li material is expected when electric vehicles become extensively used, and new battery technology is needed (Vikström et al., 2013). Minimum recycling rates of 25% by September 2012 and 45% by 2016 are required according to the EU Directive 2006/66/EC. The recycling rate will reach 50%–75%, depending on various types of the batteries (Miedema and Moll, 2013). The recycling processes of spent lithium iron phosphate batteries include thermal treatment, wet treatment, biological treatment and mechanical treatment (Al-Thyabat et al., 2013). Waste toner is dealt with by heat treatment (400°C, 600°C, and 800°C) to obtain carbon-coated ferric oxides, the structures, morphologies and electrochemical performances of which have been subsequently investigated as active materials for lithium iron batteries (Li et al., 2018). Hanisch et al. (2015) used thermal decomposition of the polyvinylidene fluoride binder to lessen the cohesion of coated active material particles and weaken the adhesion between coating and foil. Via incineration and impact liberation, 97.1% w/w of the electrode coating can be regained with aluminum impurities of only 0.1% w/w, 30 times purer than the comparative process. Fernandes et al. (2013) extracted and removed Zn(II), Fe(III), Co(II) and Ni(II) in the leachate with 2-ethylhexylphosphonic acid-2-ethylhexyl monoester (PC-88A). The lanthanide metal was extracted at pH = 1 and the recycling rate was more than 99.9%. Innocenzi and Vegliò (2012) pulverized the nickelhydrogen battery (<500μm) using 2 mol/L H2SO4 at 80 ^∘C for 3 h and 1 mol/L H2SO4 at 25 ^∘C for 1 h to obtain the leachate. The pH value was adjusted to 1.6 with NaOH and the rare earth metal was precipitated using sulfate precipitation. The resulting recycling rate was 99%, containing 64% lanthanide metal sulfate and 28% cerium sulfate. Nevertheless, the wet process requires a large amount of the strong acid, alkali, oxidant and extractant, and resulted in a large amount of waste liquid. Biological treatment with bio-acid instead of traditionally used sulfuric and nitric acids has less impact on the environment. Mishra et al. (2008) used the acidophilic Thiobacillus ferrooxidans to leach the metal in the cathode material of spent lithium iron batteries.",

          "text": "## Results and discussion\n## Effects of the airflow velocity and particle size on the air drag coefficient\nFigure 5 illustrates the velocities of the free-settling copper and aluminum fragments upon removal of electrode materials with time. It can be seen that the velocity of the copper fragments increases with time and finally converges to the maximum as shown in Figure 5(a). For d < 6 mm the larger sizes of the copper fragments lead to the larger maximum free-settling velocity and the increasing time for its convergence. For d ⩾ 6 mm, the size of the copper fragments has insignificant influence on the maximum free-settling velocity and its convergence time. For d ⩾8\n(a) mm the velocities of the different-sized copper fragments are closed and the fragments have basically the same kinematics; similarly, this also holds true for the aluminum fragments with d ⩾9 mm as shown in Figure 5(b). Figure 6 shows that the copper fragments with d ⩾5 mm as given in Figure 6(a) and the aluminum fragments with d ⩾4.5 mm with electrode materials have the basically identical kinematics for various sizes, as given in Figure 6(b).\nLetting th represent the thickness of the crushed fragment, substitution of th into Equations (12) and (22) leads to:\nwhere R1 = 0.5 CD the air drag coefficient CD is constant for 800 ⩽Rep < 2 x10 ^5 based on Equations (29) and (30) and the formulae for obtaining Relative Reynolds number; this indicates that the pneumatic separation of crushed fragments of spent lithium iron power batteries depends only on the thickness, and is independent of their particle sizes and shapes. Therefore, the copper and aluminum fragments can be effectively separated without grading.\nFigure 7 depicts the air drag coefficients of the aluminum fragments without electrode materials under various airflow velocities and particle sizes. It is revealed that the influence of the airflow velocity on the air drag coefficient only affects at the initial stage of the entrance of the crushed fragments into the pneumatic separator; the effects become insignificant when the motion of the crushed fragments becomes relatively stable. Figure 7 also gives the relation between the particle sizes of the crushed fragments and the air drag coefficients. The air drag coefficient increases with decreasing the particle size for d < 9.00 mm. The air drag coefficient converges to 0.48 for d ⩾9.00 mm; this means that the crushed fragments need not to be graded.",

          "text": "## Simple determination of the optimal airflow velocity\nThe crushed fragments are suspended if the airflow velocity equals the free-settling velocity of the fragments. Let u = 0 in Equation (21). The free-settling velocity of the crushed fragments can be determined by:\nIf v-f >0, v = - f (e ^- (rft) / (m) + 1) / (e ^- (rft) / (m) - 1) (Eq. 32)\nIf v-f⩽0, v = f (1 - e ^- (rft) / (m)) / (e ^- (rft) / (m) + 1) (Eq. 33)\nThe final free-settling velocity of the crushed fragments can be obtained by taking the limits of t for Equations (32) and (33):\nSubstituting the physical parameters into Equation (34) leads to ≈ 1.42 m/s and ≈ 1.92: m / s,, where and are the final free-settling velocities of the copper and aluminum fragments without electrode materials, respectively. Similarly, we have 2 ≈ 2.93 m/s and 2 ≈ 3.60 m/s for the crushed products with electrode materials. The allowable ranges of the airflow velocities for successfully separating copper and aluminum fragments are 1.42–1.93 m/s for the crushed products without electrodes and 2.93–3.60 m/s for the ones with electrodes. Accordingly, Equation (31) for determining the optimal airflow velocity can be rewritten as:\nuo = (vgCu + vgAl) / (2) (Eq. 35) where uo is the optimal airflow velocity. uol ≈ 1.67 m/s and uo2 ≈ 3.27 m/s can be obtained by solutions of Equation (35); these are consistent with the results from the previous calculations. Hence, the determination of the optimal airflow velocity can be remarkably simplified using the present method if the moving distance of the crushed products is considered negligible.",

          "chunk_id": "CHUNK_Pneumatic Separation and Recycling of Anode and Cathode Materials from Spent Lithium Iron Phosphate Batteries_Experimental results_f2281a78",
          "text": "## Experimental results\nAs shown in Figure 9, here a vertical pneumatic separator was used for the experiment, of which the airflow velocity ranged from 1.4 to 30 m/s and its incremental regulation 0.1–0.3 m/s. An anemograph, an electronic weigher, a ruler and scissors are also equipped. Figure 10 depicts the relations between the airflow velocity and the mass fraction of pneumatic separation products upon removal of electrodes with the particle size ranged 3–15 mm. The results observed were as follows:\n(1) All the copper fragments and most aluminum fragments moved downward and a few aluminum fragments moved upward for the airflow velocity of 1.4–1.5 m/s. The downward-moving aluminum fragments floated in the separator for a moment and then slid into the heavy-material collector along the inner wall of the pipeline.\n(2) The maximum mass fraction of aluminum in the upper separation products was 96% as shown in Figure 10(a), and that of copper in the lower separation products was 97% for the airflow velocity of 1.6–1.9 m/s, as shown in Figure 10(b).\n(3) The upper products contain partial copper and all aluminum fragments, and all the lower separation products are copper for the airflow velocity of 2.0–2.1 m/s. The results indicate that some copper fragments had “up-and-down” periods in the separator.\nTherefore, the optimal airflow velocity for the copper and aluminum fragments without electrodes is 1.6–1.9 m/s. However, it was observed that the aluminum fragments floated too long in the separator for the airflow velocity of 1.6 m/s, and some copper fragments needed several “up-and-down” periods to finally reach the collector for the airflow velocity of 1.9 m/s. The results show that the optimal airflow velocity for separating the copper and aluminum fragments without electrodes are about 1.7 m/s. Figure 11 depicts the relations between the airflow velocity and the mass fraction of pneumatic separation products without removal of electrodes with the particle size range 3–15 mm. In a similar way, we can find that the optimal airflow velocity of the copper and aluminum fragments with electrodes is about 3.3 m/s.\nthAl / thCu = 3.36 > / = 3.30 holds true for the copper and aluminum fragments with electrodes in which the aluminum fragment is once folded and the copper fragments is unfolded. The upper and lower separation products here are copper and aluminum, respectively. However, the results show that pneumatic separation of the copper and aluminum fragments is unfeasible for any air velocities because (a) the very close values of thAl / thCu and / and (b) the unevenness of the air velocity (0.1–0.3 m/s) in the separator is greater than that of the margin of the airflow velocity (0.07 m/s) of the copper and aluminum fragments. The results conclude that the uniformity and consistency of the thickness of the crushed products should be ensured when selecting the method for crushing of spent lithium iron phosphate batteries.",

Figure 11 depicts the relations between the airflow velocity and the mass fraction of pneumatic separation products without removal of electrodes with the particle size range 3–15 mm. In a similar way, we can find that the optimal airflow velocity of the copper and aluminum fragments with electrodes is about 3.3 m/s.\nthAl / thCu = 3.36 > / = 3.30 holds true for the copper and aluminum fragments with electrodes in which the aluminum fragment is once folded and the copper fragments is unfolded. The upper and lower separation products here are copper and aluminum, respectively. However, the results show that pneumatic separation of the copper and aluminum fragments is unfeasible for any air velocities because (a) the very close values of thAl / thCu and / and (b) the unevenness of the air velocity (0.1–0.3 m/s) in the separator is greater than that of the margin of the airflow velocity (0.07 m/s) of the copper and aluminum fragments. The results conclude that the uniformity and consistency of the thickness of the crushed products should be ensured when selecting the method for crushing of spent lithium iron phosphate batteries.",

          "text": "## Experimental results\nAs shown in Figure 9, here a vertical pneumatic separator was used for the experiment, of which the airflow velocity ranged from 1.4 to 30 m/s and its incremental regulation 0.1–0.3 m/s. An anemograph, an electronic weigher, a ruler and scissors are also equipped. Figure 10 depicts the relations between the airflow velocity and the mass fraction of pneumatic separation products upon removal of electrodes with the particle size ranged 3–15 mm. The results observed were as follows:\n(1) All the copper fragments and most aluminum fragments moved downward and a few aluminum fragments moved upward for the airflow velocity of 1.4–1.5 m/s. The downward-moving aluminum fragments floated in the separator for a moment and then slid into the heavy-material collector along the inner wall of the pipeline.\n(2) The maximum mass fraction of aluminum in the upper separation products was 96% as shown in Figure 10(a), and that of copper in the lower separation products was 97% for the airflow velocity of 1.6–1.9 m/s, as shown in Figure 10(b).\n(3) The upper products contain partial copper and all aluminum fragments, and all the lower separation products are copper for the airflow velocity of 2.0–2.1 m/s. The results indicate that some copper fragments had “up-and-down” periods in the separator.\nTherefore, the optimal airflow velocity for the copper and aluminum fragments without electrodes is 1.6–1.9 m/s. However, it was observed that the aluminum fragments floated too long in the separator for the airflow velocity of 1.6 m/s, and some copper fragments needed several “up-and-down” periods to finally reach the collector for the airflow velocity of 1.9 m/s. The results show that the optimal airflow velocity for separating the copper and aluminum fragments without electrodes are about 1.7 m/s. Figure 11 depicts the relations between the airflow velocity and the mass fraction of pneumatic separation products without removal of electrodes with the particle size range 3–15 mm. In a similar way, we can find that the optimal airflow velocity of the copper and aluminum fragments with electrodes is about 3.3 m/s.\nthAl / thCu = 3.36 > / = 3.30 holds true for the copper and aluminum fragments with electrodes in which the aluminum fragment is once folded and the copper fragments is unfolded. The upper and lower separation products here are copper and aluminum, respectively. However, the results show that pneumatic separation of the copper and aluminum fragments is unfeasible for any air velocities because (a) the very close values of thAl / thCu and / and (b) the unevenness of the air velocity (0.1–0.3 m/s) in the separator is greater than that of the margin of the airflow velocity (0.07 m/s) of the copper and aluminum fragments. The results conclude that the uniformity and consistency of the thickness of the crushed products should be ensured when selecting the method for crushing of spent lithium iron phosphate batteries.",

        "chunk_id": "CHUNK_Pneumatic Separation and Recycling of Anode and Cathode Materials from Spent Lithium Iron Phosphate Batteries_Introduction_c5ba2aa0_sub0",
        "section_name": "Introduction Recently, Ni-MH and lithium iron batteries as high-energy vehicle power batteries have been developed rapidly because of their advantages such as high energy density, fast charge and discharge process, long cycle life, and non-pollution attributes. However, the battery capacity decreases after hundreds of charge–discharge cycles, which finally leads to the battery being scrapped. From the viewpoint of environmental protection, conservation of natural resources and lowering the cost, battery recycling is necessary. The authors summarize the methods of Ni-MH battery and lithium iron battery recycling in domestic and foreign research, including the hydrometallurgical processing method, the pyrometallurgical processing method and the combined processing method, and each method is evaluated. An overview is then given about secondary pollution, security problems and solutions in the existing methods, which lays a foundation for future recycling of traction batteries (Yu et al., 2014). A shortage of Li material is expected when electric vehicles become extensively used, and new battery technology is needed (Vikström et al., 2013). Minimum recycling rates of 25% by September 2012 and 45% by 2016 are required according to the EU Directive 2006/66/EC. The recycling rate will reach 50%–75%, depending on various types of the batteries (Miedema and Moll, 2013). The recycling processes of spent lithium iron phosphate batteries include thermal treatment, wet treatment, biological treatment and mechanical treatment (Al-Thyabat et al., 2013). Waste toner is dealt with by heat treatment (400°C, 600°C, and 800°C) to obtain carbon-coated ferric oxides, the structures, morphologies and electrochemical performances of which have been subsequently investigated as active materials for lithium iron batteries (Li et al., 2018). Hanisch et al. (2015) used thermal decomposition of the polyvinylidene fluoride binder to lessen the cohesion of coated active material particles and weaken the adhesion between coating and foil. Via incineration and impact liberation, 97.1% w/w of the electrode coating can be regained with aluminum impurities of only 0.1% w/w, 30 times purer than the comparative process. Fernandes et al. (2013) extracted and removed Zn(II), Fe(III), Co(II) and Ni(II) in the leachate with 2-ethylhexylphosphonic acid-2-ethylhexyl monoester (PC-88A). The lanthanide metal was extracted at pH = 1 and the recycling rate was more than 99.9%. Innocenzi and Vegliò (2012) pulverized the nickelhydrogen battery (<500μm) using 2 mol/L H2SO4 at 80 ^∘C for 3 h and 1 mol/L H2SO4 at 25 ^∘C for 1 h to obtain the leachate. The pH value was adjusted to 1.6 with NaOH and the rare earth metal was precipitated using sulfate precipitation. The resulting recycling rate was 99%, containing 64% lanthanide metal sulfate and 28% cerium sulfate. Nevertheless, the wet process requires a large amount of the strong acid, alkali, oxidant and extractant, and resulted in a large amount of waste liquid. Biological treatment with bio-acid instead of traditionally used sulfuric and nitric acids has less impact on the environment. Mishra et al. (2008) used the acidophilic Thiobacillus ferrooxidans to leach the metal in the cathode material of spent lithium iron batteries.",
